## Glossary

- [Banner Ads](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-banner-ads/): Learn what mobile banner ads are, how in-app banner advertising works, recommended banner ad sizes for apps, and more.
- [Minimum Detectable Effect (MDE)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-minimum-detectable-effect-mde/): What is minimum detectable effect (MDE)? A clear guide to MDE meaning, formula, statistical power, and how to choose the right MDE for A/B tests.
- [Rewarded Video Ads](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-rewarded-video-ads/): Rewarded video ads let users watch a short video to earn rewards. See how they work in mobile apps, where to place them, and key metrics to track.
- [Ad Network](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-ad-network/): An ad network connects advertisers with apps that have ad space. Learn how ad networks work, the types, and why apps use them to show ads and earn revenue.
- [Affiliate Marketing](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-affiliate-marketing/): Affiliate marketing helps mobile apps get users at predictable cost. Partners drive installs or revenue and only get paid when results happen.
- [Data Monetization](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-data-monetization/): Discover how data monetization works in mobile apps, what types exist, how it generates revenue, and how to use it safely with consent and privacy controls.
- [Referral Marketing](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-referral-marketing/): Referral marketing helps mobile apps grow by rewarding users who invite friends. Learn how referral programs work, what to reward, and how to track conversions.
- [App Publisher](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-app-publisher/): An app publisher handles the launch, marketing, ASO, monetization, and daily growth of a mobile app, helping it gain visibility, acquire users, and increase revenue.
- [Interstitial Ads](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-interstitial-ad/): Interstitial ads are full-screen ads that appear during natural breaks in mobile apps. Learn how they work, when to use them, and how they compare to other ad formats.
- [Paywall](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-paywall/): Discover how mobile app paywalls work, where they appear, key paywall types, and best practices to increase conversions and optimize app monetization.
- [Semantic Keywords](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-semantic-keywords/): Learn what semantic keywords are in ASO and how they expand your app’s visibility by capturing related searches, user intent, and broader keyword contexts.
- [App Clips](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-app-clips/): App Clips let users access a fast, lightweight version of your iOS app without installation. Learn how App Clips work, key benefits, use cases, and FAQs.
- [Custom Store Listings](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-custom-store-listings/): Learn what custom store listings are in Google Play, how they work, key use cases, and how CSLs help improve localization, ad relevance, and app conversion rates.
- [Minimum Viable Product (MVP)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-minimum-viable-product-mvp/): Learn what a Minimum Viable Product (MVP) is, how it differs from prototypes and PoCs, and how to build an MVP for your mobile app to validate ideas faster.
- [Monthly Active Users (MAU)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-monthly-active-users-mau/): Learn what is Monthly Active Users (MAU), how to calculate it, MAU vs DAU differences, why it matters for mobile apps, and how to increase MAU through ASO.
- [Preloaded Apps](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-preloaded-apps/): What are preloaded apps? How they work on iOS and Android, which preinstalled apps you can delete or disable, and how to manage them safely.
- [Dayparting](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-dayparting/): Dayparting is the strategy of scheduling ads for specific times of day or week to maximize performance. Learn why it matters and how to use it in Apple Ads.
- [Share of Voice (SOV)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-share-of-voice-sov/): Share of Voice (SOV) measures your app’s visibility compared to competitors. Learn how it’s calculated and how to boost your presence in Apple Ads and ASO.
- [Custom Product Page](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-custom-product-page/): Custom Product Pages (CPPs) let you create tailored App Store pages with unique visuals and text. Learn how they improve ASO and Apple Ads performance.
- [Keyword Popularity](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-keyword-popularity/): Keyword Popularity shows how often a keyword is searched in the App Store or Google Play. Learn why it matters for ASO and how to use it in Apple Ads.
- [Organic Rank](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-organic-rank/): Organic rank is the natural position of an app in App Store or Google Play search results. Learn how it’s calculated and strategies to boost your visibility.
- [Installs (View-Through)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-installs-view-through/): Installs occurring after a user views an ad but doesn't click, yet later installs the app.
- [New Downloads (View-Through)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-new-downloads-view-through/): New installs that occur after a user views an ad but doesn't click, showing the ad's indirect influence on user decisions.
- [Redownloads (View-Through)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-redownloads-view-through/): Redownloads that occur after a user views an ad but doesn't click, suggesting the ad influenced their decision indirectly.
- [Installs (Total)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-installs-total/): The total number of app installs accumulated from all sources, reflecting overall app adoption and market penetration.
- [New Downloads (Total)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-new-downloads-total/): Total count of new downloads from all sources over a specific period, reflecting growth and marketing effectiveness.
- [Redownloads (Total)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-redownloads-total/): The total count of redownloads from all sources over a period, providing insights into user behavior and app appeal.
- [Average Daily Spend](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-average-daily-spend/): Average amount spent on advertising campaigns per day. Monitor it to manage budgets effectively.
- [Dimensions (Apple Ads)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-dimensions-apple-search-ads/): Attributes like keywords or locations used to segment and analyze ad performance.
- [Rank (Apple Ads)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-rank-apple-search-ads/): Your ad's position in Apple Ads results for a keyword, impacting visibility and click-through rates.
- [Match Source (Apple Ads)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-match-source-apple-search-ads/): Math Source indicates if a keyword was user-searched or suggested by Apple Ads algorithms.
- [New Downloads](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-new-downloads/): The number of first-time installs of your app by unique users, indicating success in attracting new users.
- [Redownloads](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-redownloads/): Instances where users download your app again after uninstalling, indicating regained interest or effective re-engagement.
- [Search Term](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-search-term/): Words or phrases users input when searching for apps in the app store, essential for keyword optimization.
- [Search Popularity (Apple Ads)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-search-popularity-apple-search-ads/): Optimize your Apple Ads campaigns with search popularity data. Learn how to interpret popularity scores, prioritize keywords strategically, and adjust bids based on search volume to maximize campaign efficiency.
- [Spend (Apple Ads)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-spend-apple-search-ads/): The amount spent on Apple Ads campaigns over a period, reflecting investment in app promotion.
- [Taps](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-taps/): In Apple Search Ads, a tap is counted when a user selects your ad in search results, measuring engagement.
- [First Time Deposit (FTD)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-first-time-deposit-ftd/): Learn how to increase first-time deposit (FTD) rates in gaming and betting apps. Discover proven optimization techniques, common conversion barriers, and benchmark data from top-performing apps.
- [Target Cost Per Acquisition (tCPA)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-target-cost-per-acquisition-tcpa/): A bidding strategy aiming for conversions at a set cost per acquisition, optimizing for budget efficiency.
- [Cost Per View (CPV)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-cost-per-view-cpv/): Measures cost each time a video ad is viewed by a user. Optimize CPV for better ROI.
- [View-Through Attribution](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-view-through-attribution/): Understand and implement view-through attribution for more accurate campaign measurement. Learn how it differs from click attribution, proper setup techniques, lookback window optimization, and how to interpret results.
- [Visibility](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-visibility/): Learn metadata techniques, conversion rate optimization, review management, and off-metadata strategies that improve discoverability.
- [Visibility Report](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-visibility-report/): Details how visible your app is compared to competitors across metrics, measured by a visibility score.
- [Voice Over](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-voice-over/): Audio narration used in app preview videos or tutorials to explain features, enhancing user understanding.
- [Sales Metrics](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-sales-metrics/): Data points measuring the financial performance of your app, such as revenue, ARPU, and lifetime value metrics.
- [Screenshots](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-screenshots/): Images showcasing your app's interface, used in app store listings to give users a preview of the user experience.
- [SDK (Software Development Kit)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-sdk-software-development-kit/): Learn how to evaluate, integrate, and maintain third-party SDKs in your mobile app. Discover security considerations, performance impact assessment, and implementation best practices for successful SDK integration.
- [SDK Spoofing](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-sdk-spoofing/): A type of fraud where fake data is sent to an SDK to simulate legitimate activity, undermining analytics accuracy.
- [Search Ads Visibility Score](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-search-ads-visibility-score/): Boost your app's Search Ads Visibility Score to win more impressions and reduce costs. Learn the factors affecting visibility, optimization techniques, and measurement strategies for Apple Search Ads campaigns.
- [Search by App](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-search-by-app/): A search method where users look for apps similar to ones they already use, aiding in app discovery.
- [Search by Competitor](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-search-by-competitor/): Analyzing competitor apps to inform your ASO and marketing strategies, identifying areas for improvement.
- [Search by Keyword](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-search-by-keyword/): Users search using specific keywords, making optimization crucial for discovery and attracting organic installs.
- [Search Popularity Score](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-search-popularity-score/): A metric showing how frequently users search for a keyword in the App Store, influencing ASO decisions.
- [Search Query](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-search-query/): Indicates the overall search volume of a keyword, helping determine its importance in optimization efforts.
- [Volume](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-volume/): The Volume metric offers valuable insights into an app’s discoverability through organic search.
- [Seasonality](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-seasonality/): Fluctuations in app performance due to seasonal trends or events, affecting user behavior and metrics.
- [Similar Apps](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-similar-apps/): Apps offering comparable features or content, often competing for the same user base in the app store.
- [SKAdNetwork](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-skadnetwork/): Apple's SKAdNetwork is a framework for privacy-preserving ad attribution on iOS devices, limiting data shared.
- [Store Analysis](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-store-analysis/): Examining app store data to inform marketing and ASO strategies, enhancing app visibility and performance.
- [Store Management](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-store-management/): Overseeing all aspects of your app's presence in app stores, including updates, metadata, and user engagement.
- [Tap Through Rate (TTR)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-tap-through-rate-ttr/): Boost your mobile ad performance with proven tactics to increase tap-through rates. Learn creative optimization techniques, audience targeting strategies, and placement considerations that drive higher engagement.
- [Targeted Ads](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-targeted-ads/): Advertisements aimed at specific user demographics or behaviors, increasing relevance and conversion rates.
- [Today Page](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-today-page/): A feature in the App Store showcasing editorial content and featured apps, enhancing discovery opportunities.
- [Top Ad Publishers](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-top-ad-publishers/): Publishers generating the highest revenue or impressions from in-app ads, indicating strong user engagement.
- [Top Advertisers](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-top-advertisers/): Companies spending the most on advertising within the app ecosystem, often leading in user acquisition efforts.
- [Top Apps](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-top-apps/): Apps ranking highest in the app store based on downloads or revenue, featured prominently in top charts.
- [Top Charts](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-top-charts/): Lists in app stores showcasing the most popular apps, helping users discover trending applications.
- [Top Creatives](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-top-creatives/): The most effective ad creatives generating high engagement or conversions, serving as benchmarks for success.
- [Top Developers](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-top-developers/): Developers with high-performing apps or significant market influence, recognized as top developers.
- [Top Free Apps](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-top-free-apps/): The most downloaded free apps in the app store, reflecting widespread user interest and adoption.
- [Top Grossing Apps](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-top-grossing-apps/): Uncover the monetization secrets behind the App Store's most profitable apps. Learn which revenue models they use, how they optimize conversions, and which strategies you can apply to your own app.
- [Top Paid Apps](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-top-paid-apps/): The most purchased apps requiring payment to download, often offering premium content or features.
- [Top Publishers](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-top-publishers/): Companies or individuals publishing high-ranking or high-revenue apps, considered top publishers in the market.
- [Transactional Video on Demand (TVOD)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-transactional-video-on-demand-tvod/): A model where users pay for individual pieces of video content, known as Transactional Video on Demand (TVOD).
- [Usage Data](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-usage-data/): Information on how users interact with your app; use this usage data to make improvements and enhance UX.
- [User Acquisition (UA)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-user-acquisition-ua/): Build a comprehensive user acquisition strategy for your mobile app. Compare acquisition channels, learn cost optimization techniques, and implement targeting strategies that attract high-quality users with strong LTV.
- [User Experience (UX)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-user-experience-ux/): The overall experience a user has when interacting with your app, impacting satisfaction and retention.
- [User Generated Content (UGC)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-user-generated-content-ugc/): Content created by users, influencing app reputation and engagement, often shared as reviews or media.
- [Identifier for Advertisers (IDFA)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-identifier-for-advertisers-idfa/): Unique identifier assigned to iOS devices for advertising, respecting user privacy settings.
- [Identifier for Vendor (IDFV)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-identifier-for-vendor-idfv/): Learn how IDFV works, its technical implementation, privacy compliance requirements, and how to leverage it for measurement in the privacy-first era.
- [Impression](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-impression/): An impression occurs each time an ad is displayed to a user, regardless of whether they interact with it, impacting ad visibility metrics.
- [Impression Share](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-impression-share/): Maximize your app's advertising impression share with actionable optimization strategies. Learn how to increase ad eligibility, improve quality scores, optimize bids, and outcompete rivals for valuable ad inventory.
- [In-App Ads](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-in-app-ads/): Implement effective in-app advertising that balances revenue with user satisfaction. Compare ad formats, learn placement strategies, and more!
- [In-App Bidding](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-in-app-bidding/): Monetization method where ad networks bid in real-time for ad placements within an app.
- [In-App Events](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-in-app-events/): User actions within an app, such as purchases or level completions, tracked for analytics.
- [In-App Purchase (IAP)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-in-app-purchase-iap/): Purchases made within an app that unlock additional content or features, enhancing user experience and providing revenue streams.
- [Incentivized Installs](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-incentivized-installs/): App installs that occur when users are rewarded for downloading an app. Use cautiously.
- [Indie Developer](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-indie-developer/): Learn resource-efficient development strategies, marketing approaches on limited budgets, and monetization techniques that work without publisher support.
- [Install](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-install/): An event that occurs when a user downloads and installs an app on their device, making it ready for use and engagement.
- [Install Fraud](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-install-fraud/): Fraudulent activities manipulating install metrics. Protect your campaigns from Install Fraud.
- [Install Hijacking](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-install-hijacking/): A type of fraud where malware intercepts legitimate app installs to steal attribution, impacting marketing analytics and budgets.
- [Install Referrer](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-install-referrer/): A tracking parameter that identifies where an app install originated, aiding in accurate attribution and marketing optimization.
- [Install Validation](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-install-validation/): The process of verifying that app installs are legitimate and not fraudulent, ensuring accurate analytics and protecting ad spend.
- [Instant Apps](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-instant-apps/): Lightweight app versions users can try without installing. Enhance engagement with Instant Apps.
- [Internationalization](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-internationalization/): Designing an app to support multiple languages and regional settings, allowing it to cater to a global audience effectively.
- [iOS](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-ios/): Apple's mobile operating system, iOS, used on devices like iPhones and iPads, providing the platform for app deployment and use.
- [iTunes Connect](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-itunes-connect/): Now known as App Store Connect; Apple's platform that enables developers to manage their apps, monitor performance, and access analytics.
- [Key Performance Indicator (KPI)](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-key-performance-indicator-kpi/): Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) are measurable values that indicate how effectively a company is achieving its key business objectives.
- [Keyword Cannibalization](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-keyword-cannibalization/): Prevent keyword cannibalization from hurting your app store rankings. Learn how to identify conflicting keywords across your app listings, understand the impact on visibility, and implement effective resolution tactics.
- [Keyword Density](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-keyword-density/): Learn the optimal keyword density for app store listings in 2026. Discover how to balance keyword usage, avoid over-optimization penalties, and create metadata that ranks well while remaining user-friendly.
- [Keyword Distribution](https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-keyword-distribution/): Strategic placement of keywords across app metadata fields to optimize for search algorithms and improve app discoverability.

AI Privacy Principles Mobile Action's generative AI features prioritize your privacy. The following core principles guide our approach to data handling for these features. Transparency in Data Processing Our generative AI features enhance your Mobile Action dashboard by leveraging your collected data. This provides valuable insights, analysis, and solutions. The data utilized for these features encompasses: Review Management Store Analytics Apple Ads Campaign Management No Training on Your Data without Your Consent Your data will not be used to train generative AI or ML models without your permission. You have the option to opt out of your data being used for product improvement. Mobile Action processes your data to deliver our service. This data is crucial for training and validating models that improve grouping, notifications, and overall workflow, ultimately leading to a better user experience. If you wish to contribute to the improvement of the Mobile Action service, you may opt in to allow further use of your data. Mobile Action may utilize non-identifying elements of your data for product enhancements. For instance, we might aggregate Apple Ads data to benchmark your campaign performance against a Mobile Action-developed standard. The data accessed for this benchmark is anonymized and cannot be traced back to any specific project or customer. Limited Access by Authorized Third Parties Our generative AI features utilize third-party large language models (LLMs) and are supported by the infrastructure providers listed on our subprocessor list. These subprocessors are authorized to use data strictly as directed by Mobile Action, adhering...

Security as a Company Value MobileAction Inc. ’s security & compliance principles guide how we deliver our products and services, enabling people to simply and securely access the digital world. Secure Personnel MobileAction Inc. takes the security of its data and that of its clients and customers seriously and ensures that only vetted personnel is given access to their resources. All MobileAction Inc. contractors and employees undergo background checks prior to being engaged or employed by us in accordance with local laws and industry best practices. Confidentiality or other types of Non-Disclosure Agreements (NDAs) are signed by all employees, contractors, and others who have a need to access sensitive or internal information. We embed the culture of security into our business by conducting employee security training & testing using current and emerging techniques and attack vectors. Secure Development All development projects at MobileAction Inc. , including on-premises software products, support services, and our own Digital Identity Cloud offerings follow secure development lifecycle principles. All development of new products, tools, and services, and major changes to existing ones, undergo a design review to ensure security requirements are incorporated into the proposed development. All team members that are regularly involved in any system development undergo annual secure development training in coding or scripting languages that they work with as well as any other relevant training. > Learn how Apple Ads’ expanded search results inventory changes App Store visibility, campaign scale, and search results strategy for advertisers.

- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/blog/apple-ads-expanded-search-results-inventory/

Additional ad placements in the App Store search results mark an important shift in how Apple Ads search inventory works. Starting in March 2026, search results ads will no longer appear only in the top position. They can also be shown further down the page, creating more opportunities for advertisers to appear when users are actively searching for apps. This matters because search remains one of the strongest intent signals on the App Store. Nearly 65% of downloads happen directly after a search, and top of the search results ads have historically delivered conversion rates above 60% across Apple Ads. So, it increases advertiser presence at a moment when users are already close to making a decision. For advertisers, the practical takeaway is simple: search results campaigns are becoming more scalable. If your strategy has been built around winning a single premium position, this change broadens the opportunity set. Coverage, relevance, creative fit, and budget pacing are about to matter even more. What is changing Previously, search results ads appeared only in the top position of the App Store search results. The most meaningful change is straightforward: a search results ad may now appear at the top of results or further down the page. Existing search results campaigns remain eligible for these additional positions without requiring a structural reset. Just as important, advertisers will not be able to choose or bid for a specific position. This update is simply adding additional search ad inventory within the existing Apple Ads campaign...

> Apple Ads has introduced Maximize Conversions, a new bid strategy designed for search results campaigns on the App Store.

- Published: 2026-03-16
- Modified: 2026-06-05
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/blog/maximize-conversions/

Apple Ads has introduced Maximize Conversions, a new bid strategy designed for search results campaigns on the App Store. The goal is to help advertisers generate more tap-through installs while aiming for a target CPA they define. For teams managing campaigns day to day, this update changes how bidding and discovery can be approached. Instead of adjusting keyword-level bids to reach a CPA outcome, Maximize Conversions automatically sets the optimal bid for each search query using real-time signals. Advertisers guide performance using two primary controls: target CPA and daily budget. This update arrives alongside expanded opportunities for ads in search results, which increase available inventory beyond the top position. Together, these changes can create more opportunities to scale campaigns, particularly for teams seeking broader keyword discovery and simpler campaign management. What Maximize Conversions is Maximize Conversions is a bid strategy within Apple Ads designed to maximize tap-through installs while working toward a campaign-level target CPA. Two core principles define how this strategy works: Automated bidding per search query. Bids are automatically determined for each search query. Advertisers no longer manage individual max CPT bids to influence CPA outcomes under this strategy. Search Match is included. Maximize Conversions campaigns rely on Search Match to connect ads with relevant search queries and expand coverage for keyword discovery. Advertisers still maintain some control, including the ability to add negative keywords. As with other Apple Ads campaigns, advertisers pay on a cost-per-tap basis. Charges occur when someone taps the ad on the App Store....

> Understand Google Play Store listing experiments, how to run accurate A/B tests, pick high-performing variants, and optimize your Android store listing.

- Published: 2025-12-08
- Modified: 2025-12-10
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/blog/google-play-store-listing-experiments/

Google Play store listing experiments are one of the most reliable ways to improve how your app converts store visitors into installs. Instead of guessing what title, icon, screenshots, or description will work best, you can run structured A/B tests inside Google Play Console and compare performance between variants. This helps you make decisions based on data rather than assumptions. In this blog post, you’ll learn how Google Play store listing experiments work, when to use them, how to set them up, and how to read the results. You’ll see how each part of your store listing can be tested to improve conversion rate, downloads, and visibility. What are Google Play store listing experiments? Google Play store listing experiments are A/B tests that you run inside Google Play Console to compare different versions of your store listing. You create multiple variants of a specific element, and Google Play shows those variants to new visitors, so existing users are not affected. Traffic is automatically split between variants, usually in equal distribution. Once the experiment runs long enough and has collected enough data, Google Play identifies whether a variant is likely to outperform the control. These experiments help you improve your Play Store conversion rate, install volume, and user acquisition without changing your app’s functionality. They can be done continuously, and many teams run them as part of their optimization workflow. App developers and marketers often struggle with a fundamental blind spot, knowing how many downloads an app really has. Inorganic marketing focuses on paid user acquisition (UA) channels like Apple Ads, paid social, and display networks. In most cases, app marketing teams use a mix of both. Organic channels build long-term visibility. Paid channels deliver predictable scale. It is cost-effective but it usually grows slowly and depends on factors you can’t control directly, like App Store search algorithms. Inorganic app marketing involves paid strategies like Apple Ads and social media advertising to acquire users quickly and effectively. It provides control over targeting and budgeting but requires a significant investment. The most effective approach for most app teams is to combine both organic and inorganic strategies in a hybrid marketing strategy, ensuring sustainable long-term growth and immediate visibility in a competitive market. What are custom store listings on Google Play? Custom store listings (CSLs) are alternative versions of your app’s default Google Play store listing, each aimed at a particular user segment. They allow you to change almost all the content of your store page (app name, descriptions, icon, screenshots, videos, feature graphics, etc. ) to better appeal to different audiences. All users who don’t meet a custom listing’s targeting criteria see your default listing by default, but users who do meet the criteria will see the tailored page instead. This means you can show different messaging, visuals, and promotions to different users – for example, highlighting specific features for users in a certain country or showing a special offer to users coming from a particular ad campaign. > ASO keyword cannibalization hurts your rankings by making apps fight for the same keywords. Learn 2026 fixes to boost rankings and installs.

If you’re an indie app developer or ASO agency managing multiple apps, you might be suffering from ASO keyword cannibalization without even realizing it. In 2026, the app stores are more crowded than ever, and the last thing you need is your own apps competing against each other. We will explain what ASO keyword cannibalization is, why it happens (especially when you have more than one app), how it hurts your rankings, and what you can do to fix it. What is ASO keyword cannibalization? ASO keyword cannibalization happens when your own apps unintentionally compete with each other by targeting the same keywords. In simple terms, it means multiple apps from the same developer (or agency portfolio) are using the same search terms, and thus “eating” into each other’s rankings. Picture it as siblings fighting over the TV remote — everyone loses! Instead of one app ranking strongly for a keyword, you have two or more of your apps splitting the relevance and clicks, resulting in none of them performing as well as they could. This phenomenon is like having two storefronts next to each other selling the exact same goods. You’d hope to dominate the block, but in reality you’re just dividing your customer traffic between the two. In the context of App Store Optimization (ASO), cannibalization dilutes each app’s visibility. Your app listings start competing with each other rather than against your real competitors, which can lower...

Instead of showing ads only inside third-party apps or on social media, OEM advertising leverages the phone’s built-in software and setup process. For example, an app might come pre-installed on a new Android device or be recommended to the user during the phone’s initial setup. OEM advertising is generally an Android-centric strategy (since Android OEMs like Samsung, Xiaomi, Oppo, etc. allow such placements), and it’s typically not available on the closed iOS ecosystem . Over the years, we have continuously evolved our security measures to stay ahead of threats and vulnerabilities, ensuring that we protect the valuable data of our users and enhance our systems to meet the latest global data protection standards. Today, we are proud to announce that we have achieved GDPR compliance, joining our existing SOC 2 compliance. What is GDPR? The General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) is a European Union (EU) regulation that sets guidelines for the collection and processing of personal information from individuals within the EU. GDPR came into effect on May 25, 2018, and has since been a benchmark for data protection laws globally, emphasizing transparency, security, and accountability by data controllers and processors. Why is GDPR important? GDPR fundamentally changes the landscape of data privacy. It gives individuals more control over their personal data while imposing strict rules on those hosting and processing this data anywhere in the world. The regulation aims to ensure that personal information is protected no matter where it is sent, processed, or stored. MobileAction and GDPR compliance Becoming GDPR compliant is about aligning our operations with our core values of trust and transparency. Here’s what GDPR compliance means for us at MobileAction: Enhanced data privacy: We've fine-tuned our data...

Onboarding is not an introduction; it is a guided experience in which a user perceives an app's value, receives guidance on its core features, and is confident in using the app. Thus, a well-thought-through onboarding flow can significantly increase user retention, engagement, and satisfaction. > Apple Search Ads has rolled view-through metrics that change how ad performance is measured beyond the traditional model, so how to use them?

- Published: 2024-10-16
- Modified: 2024-10-16
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/blog/apple-search-ads-introduced-view-through-metrics-so-how-can-you-use-them/

In a significant update to its platform, Apple Search Ads has rolled out view-through metrics, fundamentally enhancing how ad performance is measured beyond the traditional click-based models. This new addition to the platform helps users track the effectiveness of impressions that don't result in immediate taps but influence app installations within a one-day attribution window. App marketers can find all these new metrics on the SearchAds. com platform to ensure a richer, more detailed understanding of their advertising campaigns’ reach and cost effectiveness. However, to understand the effect of the metrics, you first need to understand what they represent. Let's take a look at what the new metrics mean for you. The impact of view-through metrics View-through metrics are designed to capture the “silent” impressions, those ad views that don't lead to immediate action but are influential enough to bring the user back to the App Store to make an installation. This addition is crucial in today's fast-paced mobile environment, where users often browse quickly and make decisions later, out of context from the initial ad viewing. By attributing app installations to the last ad a user viewed, marketers gain insight into the subtle persuasiveness of their creative content and placement strategies. Integrating these view-through metrics into SearchAds. com has involved an extensive overhaul of our analytics dashboard to ensure that data is accessible and actionable. The dashboard now displays a comprehensive suite of metrics, including traditional engagement metrics like taps and impressions, alongside the newly introduced view-through metrics. This...

> Compare in-app events and promotional content across Apple and Google: promotion types, who you can reach, deep link behavior, and approval guidelines.

In-App Events and promotional content are how you can highlight specific, time-limited activity on the App Store and Google Play. They show up as temporary promotional cards across the store, such as in search results, browse sections, editorial surfaces, and on your app’s product page, so users can see what you’re promoting before they open your app. Even though they look similar, Apple and Google built them for different goals. With App Store In-App Events, you promote a real experience happening inside your app for a limited time. Apple treats these as editorial content, so your event needs to clearly show what users can do and take them straight into that experience after they tap. With Google Play promotional content, you have more flexibility. You can promote events, major updates, or discounts, and focus on communicating value, especially to users who already have your app installed. In this guide, you’ll learn how each works, where they appear, how deep linking behaves, and how to choose the right approach so your campaigns work on both the App Store and Google Play. In-App Events vs promotional content In-App Events and promotional content are the main ways Apple and Google let you highlight timely activity directly on their app stores. While they may look similar at first glance, they work quite differently in practice. The differences mainly come down to what you can promote, who you can reach, and what happens after a user taps. Both Apple and Google confirm that your app icon is shown in multiple key places, like the App Store / Play Store listings, Home Screen, system UI, and search results on their platforms. That’s why your app icon needs to be designed intentionally, not just aesthetically. What is an app icon? In 2026, an app icon is a cross-platform trust and recognition signal that both people and the stores understand instantly. Because the icon appears across discovery feeds, Home/Launcher screens, widgets, and notifications, it’s one of your most-seen, behavior-tracked visuals and a major lever on taps and installs. Important distinction: an app icon is not the same thing as your app logo. It can be inspired by your logo, but it cannot carry tiny text, thin lines, or visual noise. Also, the shape is not controlled by you. On iOS, you submit a square icon, and the system automatically applies rounded corners and, starting in iOS 26, a Liquid Glass design. This effect gives the icon a smooth, glass-like appearance with subtle reflections and depth. On Android, you upload two layers: a foreground and a background. The system then applies an adaptive mask that adjusts the icon’s shape according to the device’s...

Mobile user acquisition is the process of getting the right people to install your app or game, and then turning those installs into activation, retention, and revenue. In practice, UA is not “more downloads. ” It’s a growth system that connects your channels (organic + paid) to your app lifecycle, so you can scale efficiently without buying low-quality users who churn. What is mobile user acquisition? Mobile user acquisition (mobile UA) is the process of attracting new users to a mobile app or game and guiding them through the app lifecycle, from install to activation, retention, and ultimately revenue. Unlike generic user acquisition, mobile UA is not measured by traffic or visits. Its success depends on whether acquired users: Install the app Complete key in-app actions (activation) Stay active over time (retention) Generate value through subscriptions, in-app purchases, or ads (LTV) Mobile UA in 2025-2026: how measurement really works Mobile user acquisition is harder to measure than it used to be. This is not just because competition increased, but because privacy rules limit how clearly you can track users, especially on iOS. As a result, UA is no longer about having perfect data. > Learn what mobile banner ads are, how in-app banner advertising works, recommended banner ad sizes for apps, and more.

- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-banner-ads/

Banner ads are one of the simplest and most common formats used in mobile app advertising. If you’re new to app monetization or just starting to learn about mobile advertising, this guide explains what they are, how they work, and when you should use them. What are banner ads? Mobile banner ads are small rectangular ad units displayed inside a mobile app. They usually appear at the top or bottom of the screen, and remain visible while the user interacts with the app. You can think of them as the mobile version of display ads on the web, but designed to fit different screen sizes and app layouts. Unlike web banners, mobile banners load through an in-app advertising SDK and follow mobile-specific size standards. How mobile banner ads work When a user opens your app, the app requests an ad from the integrated ad network. The network checks available advertisers, runs a real-time auction, and returns the best-performing ad. The ad is then rendered in the banner placement inside your app. To make this process work, you need: An SDK from an ad network (such as AdMob, AppLovin, or Unity Ads) A defined banner placement in your app’s UI A stable internet connection from the user’s device Most of this happens automatically once the SDK is integrated. > What is minimum detectable effect (MDE)? A clear guide to MDE meaning, formula, statistical power, and how to choose the right MDE for A/B tests.

- Published: 2025-12-11
- Modified: 2025-12-11
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-minimum-detectable-effect-mde/

What is the minimum detectable effect (MDE)? Minimum detectable effect (MDE) is a core concept in A/B testing and experimentation. If you run product tests, pricing tests, or mobile app store experiments, you will rely on MDE to understand whether your test can detect a meaningful change. In this glossary article, we explain what MDE is, why it matters, and how you can select the right value for your tests. Minimum detectable effect (MDE) meaning The minimum detectable effect (MDE) is the smallest change your A/B test can reliably detect. It represents the minimum effect size that must occur in the experiment for you to identify that the variant performs differently from the control. In simple terms: If the true lift is smaller than your MDE, your test may not detect it. MDE is used in: A/B testing Mobile app experiments (store listings, paywalls, onboarding, pricing, etc. ) MDE mean in A/B testing is the smallest improvement (or drop) in your chosen metric, such as conversion rate or install rate, that you can detect with statistical significance. Why MDE matters in experiments MDE is important because it directly shapes how your A/B test behaves. It affects: Sample size: A smaller MDE requires more users. A larger MDE requires fewer users. Test duration: If your MDE is too small for your traffic level, the test will take too long or never reach significance. Decision quality: If MDE is unrealistic, you risk missing meaningful changes or making decisions with weak evidence. In...

For mobile apps, an ad network helps: serve ads into placements within the app match ads to relevant users provide reporting and revenue data The ad network charges advertisers and pays publishers based on impressions, clicks, or conversions, depending on the model. Affiliate marketing and referral marketing are sometimes confused, but they are not the same. The difference comes from who is promoting the app and why. In the affiliate model, partners often run campaigns at scale across websites or channels. In referral marketing, users influence their own circles. > Learn what semantic keywords are in ASO and how they expand your app’s visibility by capturing related searches, user intent, and broader keyword contexts.

Semantic keywords help you expand your reach to the full range of searches connected to your app’s features, value, and user intent. If you’re trying to understand what is semantic keywords in ASO, think of them as a way to make your app visible in every meaningful search space around your topic, not just the main keyword you target. What are semantic keywords? Semantic keywords are search terms that are closely related in meaning, intent, or context to your main keyword. When someone searches in the App Store or Google Play, they rarely type one fixed phrase. Instead, they use variations, synonyms, and intent-driven terms. What is semantic keywords refers to these supporting terms that help search algorithms understand what your app is about. For example, if your core keyword is “meditation app”, semantic keywords can include “mindfulness app,” “guided breathing,” “stress relief,” and similar phrases. These terms don’t repeat “meditation app” exactly, but they help the store understand the full context of what your app offers. Semantic keywords vs traditional keywords Traditional keywords rely on exact matches. If your metadata includes “meditation,” you’re targeting searches for that word directly. But that approach misses related terms like “mindfulness” or “relaxation app. ” Semantic keywords broaden your discoverability by matching to queries that are topically related, even if the phrasing is different. Why semantic keywords matter in ASO? Semantic keywords are increasingly important because Google Play and App Store search algorithms try to understand intent, not just exact phrasing. When your...

What are Monthly Active Users (MAU)? Monthly Active Users (MAU) is a core user engagement metric that represents the number of unique users who engage with your app at least once within a 30-day period. In mobile growth and App Store Optimization (ASO), MAU provides directional insight into how many people return to your app after installing it. What counts as “active” can vary depending on how your product defines it, opening the app, logging in, completing a session, or performing a key action like a search or purchase. How to calculate Monthly Active Users (MAU) You calculate MAU by counting the number of unique users who performed at least one qualifying action in your app during a 30-day window. Preloaded apps, or pre-installed apps, are the apps that ship with your phone and define its default experience. They save setup time and give you reliable defaults, but they can also use storage, battery, and attention. Understanding what are preloaded apps, how preloaded apps work, and how to delete or disable the ones you don’t need, helps you keep your device clean, fast, and aligned with how you actually use it. What are preloaded apps? Preloaded apps are applications that come installed on your device the first time you turn it on. You don’t download them yourself, they’re added by the operating system provider (Apple or Google), the device manufacturer (Samsung, Xiaomi, etc. ), or sometimes your mobile carrier. You’ll also see them called preloaded apps, pre-installed apps, or system apps. In most cases, you can’t remove all of them completely, but you can usually delete or at least disable some of them. On iOS, preloaded apps include core tools like Phone, Messages, Safari, Photos, and other Apple apps that are tightly integrated into the system. On Android, preloaded apps usually include Google apps (like Chrome and Gmail), plus extra apps from the device brand and carrier. The key difference between preloaded apps and downloaded apps is that preloaded apps come installed on the device by default (often as part of the system), while downloaded apps are added by the user and can be freely installed or removed. > Dayparting is the strategy of scheduling ads for specific times of day or week to maximize performance. Learn why it matters and how to use it in Apple Ads.

- Published: 2025-09-18
- Modified: 2025-12-08
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-dayparting/

Dayparting is a digital advertising strategy that involves scheduling campaigns to run only during specific times of the day or days of the week. Instead of showing ads continuously, marketers strategically allocate their budget to the time periods when their target audience is most active or most likely to convert. In the context of Apple Ads and other mobile ad platforms, dayparting helps advertisers optimize spend, improve performance metrics such as conversion rate (CR), and avoid wasted impressions during low-activity hours. Why does Dayparting matter? Mobile users don’t behave the same way throughout the day. For example, a productivity app might see higher engagement in the mornings, while a gaming app might peak at night. Running ads continuously means you might be spending budget during hours when your audience is less engaged. Dayparting matters because it allows you to: Align with user behavior: Reach potential users when they’re most likely to download or engage with your app. Optimize ad spend: Avoid wasting budget during low-performing hours. Improve KPIs: Better click-through rates (CTR), conversions, and return on ad spend (ROAS). Support campaign testing: Compare performance across different time blocks to refine targeting. How does Dayparting work? Dayparting involves setting time-based rules in your campaign manager. Instead of running ads 24/7, you select the exact time windows and days to deliver ads. For example: Weekdays 8 AM – 10 AM: Ads for a coffee delivery app targeting office workers. Weeknights 7 PM – 11 PM: Ads for a gaming app targeting casual...

> Share of Voice (SOV) measures your app’s visibility compared to competitors. Learn how it’s calculated and how to boost your presence in Apple Ads and ASO.

- Published: 2025-09-18
- Modified: 2025-12-08
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-share-of-voice-sov/

Share of Voice (SOV) is a marketing metric that measures your brand’s visibility in comparison to competitors within a specific channel, market, or keyword set. In the context of Apple Ads and App Store Optimization (ASO), it refers to the percentage of times your app’s ad or listing is shown to users relative to the total opportunities available. In simpler terms: if there are 100 ad impressions for a keyword, and your app appears 40 times, your share of voice for that keyword is 40%. Why does Share of Voice matter? SOV provides a competitive benchmark that helps app marketers understand how much visibility they control in the marketplace. Unlike organic rank or keyword popularity, which measure individual positions or demand, SOV captures relative dominance. Key reasons it matters: Competitive insight: See whether you’re gaining or losing ground against competitors. Budget allocation: Helps identify where to spend more or less in Apple Ads campaigns. Performance measurement: Tracks the effectiveness of optimizations over time. Growth opportunities: Low SOV for high-value keywords signals untapped potential. How is Share of Voice calculated? SOV is usually expressed as a percentage: SOV = (Your Impressions ÷ Total Impressions for the Market or Keyword) × 100 For example: If your app receives 5,000 impressions out of a total 25,000 impressions for a keyword, your share of voice is 20%. If a competitor dominates with 60%, you know your visibility is significantly lower for that keyword. In mobile marketing, SOV can be calculated across: Keywords: Your share...

> Installs occurring after a user views an ad but doesn't click, yet later installs the app.

- Published: 2024-09-13
- Modified: 2025-07-03
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-installs-view-through/

Installs occurring after a user views an ad but doesn't click, yet later installs the app.

Installs (View-Through) refer to the number of new app downloads and re-downloads from users who viewed an ad without interacting with it (tapping, clicking, or converting), but then went on to install the app within a 24-hour window. This metric helps measure the impact of brand awareness and indirect influence of mobile app installs, beyond just direct clicks and taps. By analyzing view-through installs, marketers can better evaluate the halo effect of their advertising campaigns and optimize their strategies accordingly. Key Points: Counts both new app downloads and re-downloads Measures installs from users who viewed the ad but did not directly interact with it Looks at a 1-day (24-hour) window after ad impression Indicates the indirect influence of advertising on app installations Crucial for understanding the broader impact of advertising and marketing campaigns Helps evaluate the impact of brand awareness on app downloads This metric is important for mobile app marketers to track, as it provides insights into the indirect influence of advertising and the role of brand awareness in driving app installs on Apple Search Ads campaigns.

> Learn how to increase first-time deposit (FTD) rates in gaming and betting apps. Discover proven optimization techniques, common conversion barriers, and benchmark data from top-performing apps.

- Published: 2024-02-29
- Modified: 2025-12-08
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-first-time-deposit-ftd/

Learn how to increase first-time deposit (FTD) rates in gaming and betting apps. Discover proven optimization techniques, common conversion barriers, and benchmark data from top-performing apps.

What is it? First-time deposit (FTD) conversion rate is a key performance indicator (KPI) used in the online gambling industry to measure new player acquisition. It reflects the ability of an operator's marketing campaigns to convert new visitors into depositing customers. How it works? The FTD conversion rate shows what percentage of a site's total unique visitors went on to make their very first real-money deposit. A higher rate indicates greater success in driving those critical first deposits from new players. Calculation The FTD conversion rate is calculated by taking the number of unique visitors over a set period (usually monthly) and dividing it by the number of first-time deposits made in that timeframe. For example, if 10,000 people visited in January and 250 made their first deposit, the FTD rate would be 2. 5% (250/10,000). Beyond the FTD In addition to conversion rates, casino and sportsbook operators also analyze the average value of initial deposits. They look at second deposit rates as well to gauge customer retention and expected lifetime value. All of these metrics provide valuable insights for optimizing new player acquisition campaigns.

> A bidding strategy aiming for conversions at a set cost per acquisition, optimizing for budget efficiency.

- Published: 2024-02-29
- Modified: 2025-12-08
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-target-cost-per-acquisition-tcpa/

A bidding strategy aiming for conversions at a set cost per acquisition, optimizing for budget efficiency.

What is it? Target CPA bidding allows advertisers on platforms like Google Ads and Facebook to set a maximum cost they are willing to pay for each new customer acquisition from their ad campaigns. Rather than focus only on clicks or impressions, Target CPA prioritizes conversions. How it works? With Target CPA, the platform automatically adjusts bid amounts up or down based on a campaign's recorded cost-per-acquisition (CPA). The goal is to efficiently reach the target CPA set by the advertiser while maximizing the volume of new customer conversions. Calculation CPA itself is calculated by taking the total cost spent on an ad campaign and dividing it by the number of conversions (i. e. website signups, app downloads, etc. ). For instance, if a $1,000 campaign generated 100 conversions, its CPA would be $10 ($1,000/100 conversions). Target CPA bidding aims to keep the CPA right around or below the targeted amount.

> Measures cost each time a video ad is viewed by a user. Optimize CPV for better ROI.

- Published: 2024-01-25
- Modified: 2025-12-08
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-cost-per-view-cpv/

Measures cost each time a video ad is viewed by a user. Optimize CPV for better ROI.

Cost Per View (CPV) is a metric used in digital advertising to measure the cost incurred by advertisers for each view or engagement with their video content. CPV represents the total cost of advertising divided by the total number of views or engagements generated within a specific time period. CPV is primarily used in video advertising campaigns across various platforms, such as YouTube, social media, and programmatic video advertising. It helps advertisers assess the cost effectiveness of their video content in terms of user engagement and views. The formula CPV = Total Cost / Total Views Some key components Total Cost: This refers to the total amount spent on advertising within a specific time period. It includes costs such as ad spend, media buying fees, creative production costs of videos, and any other associated expenses. Total Views: This represents the total number of times a video ad is viewed or engaged with by users within the same time period. A view is typically counted when a user watches a significant portion of the video, although the exact definition may vary across platforms and campaigns. By dividing the total cost by the total number of views, we obtain the average Cost Per View. For example, if a video advertising campaign costs $2,000 and generates 10,000 views, the Cost Per View would be calculated as follows: CPV = $2,000 / 10,000 = $0. 20 In this case, the average cost per view would be $0. 20. > Understand and implement view-through attribution for more accurate campaign measurement. Learn how it differs from click attribution, proper setup techniques, lookback window optimization, and how to interpret results.

- Published: 2024-01-15
- Modified: 2025-12-08
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-view-through-attribution/

Understand and implement view-through attribution for more accurate campaign measurement. Learn how it differs from click attribution, proper setup techniques, lookback window optimization, and how to interpret results.

View-through attribution is a method used in digital advertising to measure the impact of display or video ads that are viewed by users but not directly clicked on. It attributes conversions or actions to these ads based on the user's subsequent behavior, even if they did not click on the ad at the time of viewing. In view-through attribution, a conversion is attributed to an ad if a user is exposed to the ad and later completes a desired action, such as making a purchase or signing up, without directly clicking on the ad. This is typically tracked using cookies or other tracking mechanisms that identify the user and associate their subsequent actions with the initial ad view. The concept behind view-through attribution is that even if users do not click on an ad, the exposure to the ad can still have an influence on their decision-making process and prompt them to take action later. By attributing conversions to viewed ads, advertisers can gain a more comprehensive understanding of the impact of their display or video ad campaigns. It's important to note that view-through attribution has its limitations. Since it relies on tracking mechanisms, it may not capture all instances of ad views or subsequent conversions accurately. Additionally, the length of the attribution window, i. e. , the time period between ad view and conversion, can vary and affect the attribution results. View-through attribution is often used in combination with other attribution models, such as click-through attribution, to provide a...

> Learn metadata techniques, conversion rate optimization, review management, and off-metadata strategies that improve discoverability.

A Software Development Kit, commonly referred to as an SDK, is a collection of software tools, libraries, documentation, and resources that developers use to create applications for a specific platform, framework, or software ecosystem. SDKs provide pre-built components and development tools that streamline the app development process and allow developers to leverage existing functionalities and capabilities. Some key points Components and Libraries: SDKs typically include a set of components and libraries that developers can use to build applications. These components may include pre-written code modules, APIs (Application Programming Interfaces), frameworks, UI elements, and other software resources. By using these pre-built components, developers can save time and effort by not having to build everything from scratch. Development Tools: SDKs often come bundled with development tools that assist developers in creating, testing, debugging, and deploying applications. These tools may include integrated development environments (IDEs), compilers, debuggers, emulators or simulators, and other utilities. SDKs aim to provide a complete development environment that streamlines the development workflow. Platform Specificity: SDKs are typically designed for specific platforms, operating systems, or software ecosystems. For example, there are SDKs tailored for mobile app development on iOS and Android, web development with specific programming languages or frameworks, game development for consoles or PC, and many more. Each SDK provides platform-specific features, APIs, and resources to facilitate app development for that particular platform. > A type of fraud where fake data is sent to an SDK to simulate legitimate activity, undermining analytics accuracy.

- Published: 2024-01-14
- Modified: 2025-12-08
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-sdk-spoofing/

A type of fraud where fake data is sent to an SDK to simulate legitimate activity, undermining analytics accuracy.

SDK spoofing, also known as Software Development Kit spoofing, is a fraudulent technique used to deceive mobile applications or advertising networks by falsifying data or interactions with a software development kit (SDK). SDKs are commonly used by developers to integrate third-party services, such as analytics, advertising, or user tracking, into their mobile applications. How it works? Manipulating SDK Signals: SDK spoofing involves manipulating the signals or data sent by the SDK to create the illusion of user engagement or activity. This can include generating fake ad impressions, clicks, app installs, or other interactions that advertisers or app developers typically use to measure user engagement or monetization. Faking Device Information: SDK spoofing may involve providing false device information, such as device IDs, IP addresses, or geolocation data, to mimic legitimate user activity. By spoofing these parameters, fraudsters attempt to make their actions appear authentic and bypass fraud detection systems. Automated Bot Activity: SDK spoofing often relies on automated bot activity to generate fake interactions with the SDK. Bots can simulate user behavior, such as clicking on ads, installing apps, or generating app usage data. This artificially inflates engagement metrics and can deceive advertisers into thinking they are reaching real users. Fraudulent Monetization: SDK spoofing can be used to fraudulently generate revenue for malicious actors. By simulating ad impressions, clicks, or app installs, fraudsters can exploit advertising networks or app developers and receive payouts for non-existent or fraudulent user engagement. Negative impacts Financial Loss: Advertisers may spend their advertising budgets on non-existent...

> Apple's SKAdNetwork is a framework for privacy-preserving ad attribution on iOS devices, limiting data shared.

- Published: 2024-01-14
- Modified: 2025-12-08
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-skadnetwork/

Apple's SKAdNetwork is a framework for privacy-preserving ad attribution on iOS devices, limiting data shared.

Description SKAdNetwork is an attribution framework developed by Apple for mobile app advertising on iOS devices. It provides a privacy-focused solution for measuring the effectiveness of ad campaigns while preserving user privacy. SKAdNetwork allows advertisers to track app installations and post-install events without revealing individual user-level data to third-party ad networks or advertisers. Why does it matter? SKAdNetwork is significant in the mobile app advertising industry due to increasing privacy concerns and regulations such as the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) and Apple's App Tracking Transparency (ATT) framework. It enables advertisers to measure the impact of their ad campaigns accurately while respecting user privacy and complying with privacy regulations. By using SKAdNetwork, advertisers can make data-driven decisions, optimize their campaigns, and allocate their advertising budgets effectively. How it is used? SKAdNetwork is utilized in the following ways in the mobile app advertising industry: Attribution: SKAdNetwork provides a privacy-centric attribution model that allows advertisers to measure the effectiveness of their ad campaigns. It assigns a unique, privacy-preserving identifier called the "conversion value" to each ad impression. The conversion value indicates specific post-install events or actions taken by the user within the app, such as completing a purchase or reaching a certain level in a game. Advertisers can access aggregated campaign performance data without accessing individual user-level data. Campaign Optimization: Advertisers can leverage the data provided by SKAdNetwork to optimize their campaigns. By analyzing the performance of different ad networks, publishers, or creative variations, advertisers can identify which sources or strategies are...

TTR is used in the following ways in digital advertising: Ad Performance Evaluation: By tracking TTR, advertisers can evaluate the performance of their ads and creative variations. They can compare the TTR across different ad formats, designs, messaging, or placements to identify which elements are resonating most effectively with the target audience. This information helps optimize future ad campaigns and improve overall campaign performance. Ad Placement Optimization: TTR can provide insights into the effectiveness of ad placements on various platforms, websites, or within apps. Advertisers can analyze TTR data to determine which placements generate higher engagement and adjust their media buying strategies accordingly. > A model where users pay for individual pieces of video content, known as Transactional Video on Demand (TVOD).

- Published: 2024-01-14
- Modified: 2025-12-08
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-transactional-video-on-demand-tvod/

A model where users pay for individual pieces of video content, known as Transactional Video on Demand (TVOD).

Transactional Video on Demand (TVOD) is a business model in the media and entertainment industry that allows users to rent or purchase individual video content on a pay-per-view basis. It is a digital distribution method where users have the option to access specific video content, such as movies, TV shows, or other forms of digital media, for a specified fee. In TVOD, users typically have the flexibility to choose the content they want to watch and pay for it separately. The payment can be in the form of a one-time rental fee or a purchase price. Once the payment is made, users are granted access to the content for a limited period (in the case of rentals) or indefinitely (in the case of purchases). TVOD platforms or services enable users to access a wide range of video content on-demand, giving them the freedom to watch what they want, when they want. This model is often used for recently released movies, premium or exclusive content, or niche content that may not be available through other distribution channels. TVOD differs from subscription-based models like Subscription Video on Demand (SVOD), where users pay a recurring fee to access a library of content. With TVOD, users have more control over their viewing choices and only pay for the specific content they wish to consume. TVOD services can be accessed through various platforms, including streaming devices, smart TVs, mobile apps, or web browsers. Examples of TVOD platforms include Apple iTunes, Google Play Movies & TV,...

User demographics: Information about the age, gender, location, or other characteristics of app users. In-app purchases: Data related to the number and value of purchases made within the app, including subscriptions or one-time purchases. User engagement: Metrics such as the number of interactions, actions, or events performed by users within the app. Crash reports: Information about app crashes or errors encountered by users, helping developers identify and fix issues. Conversion rates: The percentage of users who complete a specific action or goal within the app, such as signing up or making a purchase. > Unique identifier assigned to iOS devices for advertising, respecting user privacy settings.

- Modified: 2025-12-08
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-identifier-for-advertisers-idfa/

Unique identifier assigned to iOS devices for advertising, respecting user privacy settings.

The Identifier for Advertisers (IDFA) is a unique device identifier assigned to Apple devices, such as iPhones and iPads. It is used by advertisers and app developers to track user interactions, deliver personalized advertisements, and measure the effectiveness of advertising campaigns on iOS devices. Why does it matter? IDFA plays a crucial role in mobile advertising by providing a persistent identifier that allows advertisers to target specific users, track their activities across different apps, and deliver personalized advertising experiences. It enables advertisers to understand user behavior, optimize ad targeting, and measure the performance of their campaigns. Some key points Here are some key points regarding IDFA: Uniqueness and Persistence: Each iOS device is assigned a unique IDFA, which remains consistent across apps and sessions unless explicitly reset by the user. This persistent identifier allows advertisers to recognize and track individual users over time. Ad Targeting and Personalization: IDFA enables advertisers to deliver personalized ads based on user interests, demographics, and behavioral data. Advertisers can use IDFA to segment users and serve relevant ads that align with their preferences and browsing history. Attribution and Conversion Tracking: IDFA is used for attribution and conversion tracking purposes. Advertisers can link ad impressions and clicks to specific actions, such as app installations or in-app purchases, to measure the effectiveness of their advertising campaigns. User Privacy and App Tracking Transparency (ATT): In response to growing privacy concerns, Apple introduced the App Tracking Transparency (ATT) framework. Starting with iOS 14. 5, users must explicitly grant permission...

> Learn how IDFV works, its technical implementation, privacy compliance requirements, and how to leverage it for measurement in the privacy-first era.

- Published: 2024-01-11
- Modified: 2025-12-08
- URL: https://www.mobileaction.co/glossary/what-is-identifier-for-vendor-idfv/

Learn how IDFV works, its technical implementation, privacy compliance requirements, and how to leverage it for measurement in the privacy-first era.

The Identifier for Vendor (IDFV) is a unique identifier assigned to iOS devices by Apple. It is specific to a particular vendor or developer and remains consistent across all apps from that vendor on a specific device. The IDFV is designed to provide a persistent identifier that allows vendors to track user activity and behavior within their own apps, but it does not allow cross-app tracking or sharing of data between different vendors. Some key points Here are some key points regarding IDFV: Vendor-Specific Identifier: IDFV is specific to a particular vendor or developer. It allows vendors to distinguish their own apps on a given device and track user activity and behavior within their app ecosystem. Consistency Across Apps: IDFV remains the same for all apps developed by a particular vendor on a specific device. This consistency enables vendors to track user interactions and behavior across their own apps on a device. Limited to a Vendor's Apps: IDFV does not provide cross-app tracking capabilities. It is limited to tracking activity within a vendor's own apps and does not allow the sharing of IDFV data or user information between different vendors. User Privacy Considerations: IDFV is subject to user privacy considerations. It is essential for vendors to handle IDFV data responsibly, adhere to privacy regulations, and respect user preferences regarding data collection and usage. Usage and benefits IDFV can be utilized by vendors for various purposes, including: User Analytics: IDFV allows vendors to analyze user behavior, engagement, and usage patterns across...

By combining impression data with other metrics like CTR and conversions, marketers can create well-rounded, effective campaigns that resonate with their target audience and achieve long-term growth goals. What is an Impression? An impression in mobile marketing refers to the moment an advertisement or piece of content is displayed on a user’s screen. Unlike clicks or interactions, impressions are strictly about visibility, signifying that the content has been presented to the audience, regardless of whether they engage with it. For instance, in the context of mobile app marketing, an impression might occur when your app's advertisement appears within a user’s social media feed, on a search engine results page, or inside another app. It’s a fundamental metric used to measure the reach and exposure of marketing campaigns, especially in performance marketing and brand awareness efforts. Some key points Display and Visibility: An impression is generated when an ad is successfully displayed, either partially or fully, in a user's viewable area. It can occur on websites, mobile apps, social media platforms, search engine results pages, or other digital channels where ads are served. Counting and Measurement: Impressions are counted and measured to determine the reach and exposure of an ad campaign. Impression share is a metric used in online advertising that represents the percentage of impressions an advertiser receives out of the total available impressions in a specific market or target audience. It provides insights into the level of exposure and visibility an advertiser achieves compared to their potential reach. Some key points Definition: Impression share is the ratio of the number of impressions an advertiser's ad receives to the total number of impressions available for the selected targeting criteria, such as keywords, placements, or audience segments. It is typically expressed as a percentage. Market Coverage: Impression share helps advertisers understand how effectively their ads are reaching their target audience within a particular market or advertising platform. A higher impression share indicates that an advertiser's ads are being displayed more frequently relative to the competition. Ad Rank and Position: Impression share is influenced by factors such as ad rank, bid strategy, quality score, and budget allocation. Advertisers with higher ad ranks and budgets are more likely to achieve a higher impression share and secure better ad positions. Competitor Analysis: By monitoring impression share, advertisers can assess their performance relative to competitors. It provides insights into market competitiveness and opportunities for improving ad strategy and budget allocation. Lost Impression Share: Lost impression share is the portion of available impressions that an advertiser did not receive due to various reasons, such as budget constraints, low ad rank, or targeting limitations. In-app ads are advertisements that are displayed within a mobile application (app). They are a popular form of monetization for app developers and publishers, allowing them to generate revenue by displaying ads to their users while they are engaged with the app. In-app ads come in various formats and can be targeted based on user demographics, behavior, and interests. Some key points Ad Formats: In-app ads can take different formats, including banner ads, interstitial ads, native ads, rewarded ads, and video ads. Banner ads are typically displayed at the top or bottom of the app screen, while interstitial ads are full-screen ads that appear between app content. Native ads are designed to blend seamlessly with the app's user interface, while rewarded ads offer users incentives, such as in-app currency or extra content, in exchange for viewing the ad. Video ads are typically short video clips that can be skippable or non-skippable. Monetization Strategy: In-app ads serve as a primary monetization strategy for many app developers and publishers. By displaying ads, they can generate revenue either through impressions (CPM - cost per thousand impressions) or user interactions (CPC - cost per click) with the ads. Advertisers pay the app developer or publisher for the ad space, and the revenue is shared between the app developer and the ad network or platform. Targeting Capabilities: In-app ads can be targeted to specific user segments based on various factors, such as demographics, location, interests, and previous app usage behavior.
